\noindent This document contains all 70 \emph{Logarithms} questions, each with a fully worked
solution and a TikZ graph. Each diagram plots the relevant logarithmic curve \(y=\log_b x\) (or its inverse
exponential): the \(x\)-intercept \((1,0)\) and key points are marked, the vertical asymptote \(x=0\) is shown,
a dashed reference line passes through the solution for equation questions, and the line \(y=x\) is added for
inverse-function pairs.

\section*{Logarithms 01\quad\small\texttt{(el2-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 01 (el2-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Write the following using logarithm notation: \( 3^4 = 81 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Use the rule: if \( a^c = b \) then \( \log_a b = c \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^4 = 81 \Rightarrow \log_3 81 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
The base is 3, the power is 4, and the result is 81.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_3 81 = 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 02\quad\small\texttt{(el2-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 02 (el2-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Write the following using logarithm notation: \( 5^3 = 125 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Apply \( a^c = b \Leftrightarrow \log_a b = c \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^3 = 125 \Rightarrow \log_5 125 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Base 5, power 3, result 125.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_5 125 = 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 03\quad\small\texttt{(el2-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 03 (el2-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write the following using logarithm notation: \( 7^2 = 49 \), \( 4^{-2} = \dfrac{1}{16} \), and \( 27^{\frac{1}{3}} = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Convert \( 7^2 = 49 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_7 49 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Base 7, power 2.

\textit{Step 2: Convert \( 4^{-2} = \frac{1}{16} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4 \frac{1}{16} = -2
\end{gathered}\]
The negative power gives a fractional result.

\textit{Step 3: Convert \( 27^{\frac{1}{3}} = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{27} 3 = \frac{1}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
A power of \( \frac{1}{3} \) is a cube root.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_7 49 = 2 \); \(\displaystyle  \log_4 \frac{1}{16} = -2 \); \(\displaystyle  \log_{27} 3 = \frac{1}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 04\quad\small\texttt{(el2-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 04 (el2-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write the following using index notation (do not evaluate): \( \log_4 m = 3 \), \( \ln p = 7 \), and \( \log k = 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Convert \( \log_4 m = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^3 = m
\end{gathered}\]
Base 4 raised to power 3 equals \( m \).

\textit{Step 2: Convert \( \ln p = 7 \). Recall \( \ln \) means \( \log_e \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^7 = p
\end{gathered}\]
The natural log has base \( e \).

\textit{Step 3: Convert \( \log k = 5 \). Recall \( \log \) without a base means \( \log_{10} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
10^5 = k
\end{gathered}\]
Base 10 is assumed when no base is written.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  4^3 = m \); \(\displaystyle  e^7 = p \); \(\displaystyle  10^5 = k \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 05\quad\small\texttt{(el2-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 05 (el2-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write the following using index notation: \( \log_a b = n \), \( \ln q = r \), and \( \log_6 w = z \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( \log_a b = n \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
a^n = b
\end{gathered}\]
General form of the log-to-index conversion.

\textit{Step 2: \( \ln q = r \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^r = q
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) has base \( e \).

\textit{Step 3: \( \log_6 w = z \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6^z = w
\end{gathered}\]
Base 6 raised to \( z \) gives \( w \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  a^n = b \); \(\displaystyle  e^r = q \); \(\displaystyle  6^z = w \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Logarithms 06\quad\small\texttt{(el2-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 06 (el2-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the value of each of the following:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \log_2 32 \) \quad  (b) \( \log_3 81 \) \quad  (c) \( \log_5 5 \) \quad  (d) \( \log_7 1 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): What power do you raise 2 to in order to get 32?}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^5 = 32 \Rightarrow \log_2 32 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2^1=2, 2^2=4, 2^3=8, 2^4=16, 2^5=32 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( 3^4 = 81 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 81 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3^4 = 81 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( \log_a a = 1 \) for any valid base.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5 5 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Any number raised to the power 1 equals itself.

\textit{Step 4: Part (d): \( \log_a 1 = 0 \) for any valid base.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_7 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( 7^0 = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (a) 5 \quad (b) 4 \quad (c) 1 \quad (d) 0\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 07\quad\small\texttt{(el2-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 07 (el2-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the value of each of the following:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \log_4 64 \) \quad  (b) \( \log_6 36 \) \quad  (c) \( \log_3 \dfrac{1}{9} \) \quad  (d) \( \log_2 \dfrac{1}{8} \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( 4^3 = 64 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4 64 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^3 = 64 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( 6^2 = 36 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_6 36 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( 6^2 = 36 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( \frac{1}{9} = 3^{-2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 \frac{1}{9} = -2
\end{gathered}\]
A negative logarithm comes from a reciprocal.

\textit{Step 4: Part (d): \( \frac{1}{8} = 2^{-3} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 \frac{1}{8} = -3
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2^{-3} = \frac{1}{8} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 3 \quad  (b) 2 \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  -2 \) \quad  (d) \(\displaystyle  -3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 08\quad\small\texttt{(el2-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 08 (el2-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the value of each of the following:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \log_9 3 \) \quad  (b) \( \log_8 2 \) \quad  (c) \( \log_{16} 4 \) \quad  (d) \( \log_{32} 2 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( 3 = 9^{1/2} \) (square root of 9).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_9 3 = \frac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 9^{1/2} = \sqrt{9} = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( 2 = 8^{1/3} \) (cube root of 8).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_8 2 = \frac{1}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 8^{1/3} = \sqrt[3]{8} = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( 4 = 16^{1/2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{16} 4 = \frac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \sqrt{16} = 4 \).

\textit{Step 4: Part (d): \( 2 = 32^{1/5} \) (fifth root of 32).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{32} 2 = \frac{1}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 32^{1/5} = \sqrt[5]{32} = 2 \) since \( 2^5 = 32 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \frac{1}{2} \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  \frac{1}{3} \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  \frac{1}{2} \) \quad  (d) \(\displaystyle  \frac{1}{5} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 09\quad\small\texttt{(el2-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 09 (el2-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Use a calculator to find each of the following, rounding to 3 decimal places:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \log 50 \) \quad  (b) \( \log 0.4 \) \quad  (c) \( \ln 5 \) \quad  (d) \( \ln 100 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( \log 50 = \log_{10} 50 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 50 = 1.699
\end{gathered}\]
\( 10^{1.699} \approx 50 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( \log 0.4 \) --- result is negative since \( 0.4 < 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 0.4 = -0.398
\end{gathered}\]
\( 10^{-0.398} \approx 0.4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( \ln 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 5 = 1.609
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{1.609} \approx 5 \).

\textit{Step 4: Part (d): \( \ln 100 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 100 = 4.605
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{4.605} \approx 100 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  1.699 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  -0.398 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  1.609 \) \quad  (d) \(\displaystyle  4.605 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 10\quad\small\texttt{(el2-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 10 (el2-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the value of \( x \) (where \( x > 0 \)) in each of the following:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \log_x 64 = 3 \) \quad  (b) \( \log_x 100 = 2 \) \quad  (c) \( \log_x 625 = 4 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Convert to index form: \( x^3 = 64 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \sqrt[3]{64} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^3 = 64 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( x^2 = 100 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \sqrt{100} = 10
\end{gathered}\]
\( 10^2 = 100 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( x^4 = 625 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \sqrt[4]{625} = 5
\end{gathered}\]
\( 5^4 = 625 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = 10 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  x = 5 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 11\quad\small\texttt{(el2-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 11 (el2-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the value of \( x \) (where \( x > 0 \)) by writing each equation in index notation:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \log_3 x = 4 \) \quad  (b) \( \log_5 x = 3 \) \quad  (c) \( \log_2 x = 6 \) \quad  (d) \( \log_4 x = -1 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( \log_3 x = 4 \Rightarrow x = 3^4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 81
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3^4 = 81 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( \log_5 x = 3 \Rightarrow x = 5^3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 125
\end{gathered}\]
\( 5^3 = 125 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( \log_2 x = 6 \Rightarrow x = 2^6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 64
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2^6 = 64 \).

\textit{Step 4: Part (d): \( \log_4 x = -1 \Rightarrow x = 4^{-1} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{1}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{-1} = \frac{1}{4} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 81 \quad  (b) 125 \quad  (c) 64 \quad  (d) \(\displaystyle  \frac{1}{4} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 12\quad\small\texttt{(el2-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 12 (el2-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Without a calculator, explain why \( \log_5 1 = 0 \) and \( \log_5 5 = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( \log_5 1 = 0 \) because \( 5^0 = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^0 = 1 \Rightarrow \log_5 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Any positive base raised to the power zero gives 1.

\textit{Step 2: \( \log_5 5 = 1 \) because \( 5^1 = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^1 = 5 \Rightarrow \log_5 5 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Any base raised to the power 1 equals itself.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_5 1 = 0 \) (since \(\displaystyle  5^0=1 \)) and \(\displaystyle  \log_5 5 = 1 \) (since \(\displaystyle  5^1=5 \)).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 13\quad\small\texttt{(el2-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 13 (el2-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{State the values of: \( \ln e \), \( \ln 1 \), and \( \ln e^5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( \ln e = \log_e e = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln e = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Because \( e^1 = e \).

\textit{Step 2: \( \ln 1 = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Because \( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: \( \ln e^5 = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln e^5 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Using the inverse property: \( \ln(e^x) = x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \ln e = 1 \); \(\displaystyle  \ln 1 = 0 \); \(\displaystyle  \ln e^5 = 5 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 14\quad\small\texttt{(el2-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 14 (el2-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( e^x = 7 \), giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution correct to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln e^x = \ln 7 \Rightarrow x = \ln 7
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) and \( e \) are inverse functions, so \( \ln e^x = x \).

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate to 3 s.f.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 7 \approx 1.95
\end{gathered}\]
Using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 7 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 1.95 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 15\quad\small\texttt{(el2-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 15 (el2-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve each equation, giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( e^x = 15 \) \quad  (b) \( e^x = 0.3 \) \quad  (c) \( 3e^x = 24 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 15 \approx 2.71
\end{gathered}\]
Direct application of \( \ln \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( e^x = 0.3 \). Since \( 0.3 < 1 \), the answer is negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 0.3 \approx -1.20
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) of a number between 0 and 1 is negative.

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Divide by 3 first.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 8 \Rightarrow x = \ln 8 \approx 2.08
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate \( e^x \) before taking \( \ln \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 15 \approx 2.71 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 0.3 \approx -1.20 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 8 \approx 2.08 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 16\quad\small\texttt{(el2-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 16 (el2-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve each equation, giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( e^x = 430 \) \quad  (b) \( 4e^x = 36 \) \quad  (c) \( e^x = 0.05 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( x = \ln 430 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 430 \approx 6.06
\end{gathered}\]
Direct application.

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Divide both sides by 4 to get \( e^x = 9 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 9 \approx 2.20
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange before applying \( \ln \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( e^x = 0.05 \). Result is negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 0.05 \approx -3.00
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(0.05) = \ln(\frac{1}{20}) \approx -2.996 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 430 \approx 6.06 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 9 \approx 2.20 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 0.05 \approx -3.00 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 17\quad\small\texttt{(el2-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 17 (el2-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \ln x = 4 \), giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply \( e \) to both sides to undo the \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{\ln x} = e^4 \Rightarrow x = e^4
\end{gathered}\]
\( e \) and \( \ln \) are inverse functions.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate to 3 s.f.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^4 \approx 54.6
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^4 \approx 54.598 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = e^4 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 54.6 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 18\quad\small\texttt{(el2-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 18 (el2-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve each equation, giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \ln x = 6 \) \quad  (b) \( \ln x = 0.8 \) \quad  (c) \( 4\ln x = 20 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Raise \( e \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^6 \approx 403
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^6 \approx 403.4 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( x = e^{0.8} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^{0.8} \approx 2.23
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{0.8} \approx 2.2255 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Divide by 4 first to get \( \ln x = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^5 \approx 148
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^5 \approx 148.4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = e^6 \approx 403 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = e^{0.8} \approx 2.23 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  x = e^5 \approx 148 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 19\quad\small\texttt{(el2-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 19 (el2-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve each equation, giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \ln x = -2 \) \quad  (b) \( 5\ln x = 30 \) \quad  (c) \( \ln 3x = 7 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( \ln x = -2 \Rightarrow x = e^{-2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^{-2} = \frac{1}{e^2} \approx 0.135
\end{gathered}\]
A negative result for \( \ln x \) means \( 0 < x < 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( 5\ln x = 30 \Rightarrow \ln x = 6 \Rightarrow x = e^6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^6 \approx 403
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 5 first.

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( \ln 3x = 7 \Rightarrow 3x = e^7 \Rightarrow x = \dfrac{e^7}{3} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{e^7}{3} \approx 366
\end{gathered}\]
Raise \( e \) to both sides, then divide by 3.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = e^{-2} \approx 0.135 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = e^6 \approx 403 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^7}{3} \approx 366 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 20\quad\small\texttt{(el2-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 20 (el2-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve each equation, giving (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( \ln(x - 1) = 3 \) \quad  (b) \( \ln(2x + 1) = 5 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Raise \( e \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 1 = e^3 \Rightarrow x = e^3 + 1 \approx 21.1
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^3 \approx 20.09 \), so \( x \approx 21.1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Raise \( e \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 = e^5 \Rightarrow 2x = e^5 - 1 \Rightarrow x = \frac{e^5 - 1}{2} \approx 73.7
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^5 \approx 148.41 \), so \( \frac{148.41 - 1}{2} \approx 73.7 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = e^3 + 1 \approx 21.1 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^5-1}{2} \approx 73.7 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 21\quad\small\texttt{(el2-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 21 (el2-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Without a calculator, simplify:  \\[3pt]  (a) \( e^{\ln 9} \) \quad  (b) \( \ln(e^{12}) \) \quad  (c) \( e^{\ln x} \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( e \) and \( \ln \) are inverse functions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{\ln 9} = 9
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{\ln a} = a \) for any \( a > 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( \ln(e^{12}) = 12 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(e^{12}) = 12
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(e^x) = x \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( e^{\ln x} = x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{\ln x} = x
\end{gathered}\]
The general inverse property.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 9 \quad  (b) 12 \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  x \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 22\quad\small\texttt{(el2-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 22 (el2-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The graph of \( y = \ln x \) is a reflection of \( y = e^x \) in the line \( y = x \).  \\[3pt]  (a) State the coordinates where \( y = \ln x \) crosses the \( x \)-axis.  \\[3pt]  (b) State the equation of the asymptote of \( y = \ln x \).  \\[3pt]  (c) State the domain of \( y = \ln x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): \( \ln x = 0 \Rightarrow x = e^0 = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(1,\ 0)
\end{gathered}\]
The graph crosses the \( x \)-axis at \( x = 1 \) since \( \ln 1 = 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( \ln x \to -\infty \). The asymptote is the \( y \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The graph approaches but never touches \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): \( \ln x \) is only defined for positive \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x > 0
\end{gathered}\]
You cannot take the logarithm of zero or a negative number.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  (1, 0) \). (b) \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \). (c) \(\displaystyle  x > 0 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 23\quad\small\texttt{(el2-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 23 (el2-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given that \( \log_3 x = 2 \) and \( \log_3 y = 6 \), write \( y \) in terms of \( x \) using index notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Convert \( \log_3 x = 2 \) to index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3^2 \Rightarrow 3 = x^{1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Express 3 in terms of \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Convert \( \log_3 y = 6 \) to index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3^6
\end{gathered}\]
Express \( y \) using base 3.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( 3 = x^{1/2} \) into \( y = 3^6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \left(x^{1/2}\right)^6 = x^3
\end{gathered}\]
Use the laws of indices: \( (x^{1/2})^6 = x^3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y = x^3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 24\quad\small\texttt{(el2-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 24 (el2-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given that \( \log_2 x = 4 \) and \( \log_2 y = 12 \), write \( y \) in terms of \( x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: From \( \log_2 x = 4 \): \( x = 2^4 \Rightarrow 2 = x^{1/4} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2 = x^{1/4}
\end{gathered}\]
Raise both sides to the power \( \frac{1}{4} \).

\textit{Step 2: From \( \log_2 y = 12 \): \( y = 2^{12} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2^{12}
\end{gathered}\]
Index form.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( 2 = x^{1/4} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \left(x^{1/4}\right)^{12} = x^{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{12}{4} = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y = x^3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 25\quad\small\texttt{(el2-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 25 (el2-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given that \( \ln x = 3 \) and \( \ln y = 9 \), write \( y \) in terms of \( x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: From \( \ln x = 3 \): \( x = e^3 \Rightarrow e = x^{1/3} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e = x^{1/3}
\end{gathered}\]
Raise both sides to \( \frac{1}{3} \).

\textit{Step 2: From \( \ln y = 9 \): \( y = e^9 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^9
\end{gathered}\]
Index form.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( e = x^{1/3} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \left(x^{1/3}\right)^9 = x^3
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{9}{3} = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y = x^3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 26\quad\small\texttt{(el2-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 26 (el2-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given that \( \ln x = 4 \) and \( \ln y = 2 \), write \( y \) in terms of \( x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: From \( \ln x = 4 \): \( x = e^4 \Rightarrow e = x^{1/4} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e = x^{1/4}
\end{gathered}\]
Raise both sides to \( \frac{1}{4} \).

\textit{Step 2: From \( \ln y = 2 \): \( y = e^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^2
\end{gathered}\]
Index form.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( e = x^{1/4} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \left(x^{1/4}\right)^2 = x^{1/2} = \sqrt{x}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{2}{4} = \frac{1}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y = \sqrt{x} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 27\quad\small\texttt{(el2-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 27 (el2-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 7e^x = 35 \). Give (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide both sides by 7.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate the exponential term.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 5 \approx 1.61
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln 5 \approx 1.609 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 5 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 1.61 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 28\quad\small\texttt{(el2-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 28 (el2-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 6\ln x = 42 \). Give (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide both sides by 6.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln x = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate the \( \ln x \) term.

\textit{Step 2: Raise \( e \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^7 \approx 1100
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^7 \approx 1096.6 \), which rounds to \( 1100 \) to 3 s.f.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = e^7 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 1100 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 29\quad\small\texttt{(el2-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 29 (el2-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(5x) = 3 \). Give (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Raise \( e \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
5x = e^3
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{\ln(5x)} = 5x \).

\textit{Step 2: Divide by 5.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{e^3}{5} \approx 4.02
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^3 \approx 20.09 \Rightarrow \frac{20.09}{5} \approx 4.02 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^3}{5} \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 4.02 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 30\quad\small\texttt{(el2-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 30 (el2-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2e^{x+1} = 14 \). Give (i) an exact solution and (ii) a solution to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide both sides by 2.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{x+1} = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate the exponential.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 1 = \ln 7
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln e^{x+1} = x+1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Subtract 1.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 7 - 1 \approx 0.946
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln 7 \approx 1.946 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 7 - 1 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 0.946 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 31\quad\small\texttt{(el2-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 31 (el2-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{(a) Sketch the graph of \( y = \ln x \), labelling the \( x \)-intercept and the asymptote.  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (b) On the same axes, sketch \( y = \ln(x - 2) \). State its \( x \)-intercept and asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Key features of \( y = \ln x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-intercept: } (1, 0); \quad \text{asymptote: } x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln 1 = 0 \); as \( x \to 0^+ \), \( \ln x \to -\infty \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): \( y = \ln(x-2) \) is \( y = \ln x \) shifted 2 units to the right.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\text{-intercept: set } \ln(x-2)=0 \Rightarrow x-2=1 \Rightarrow x=3 \Rightarrow (3,0)
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x \)-intercept shifts from 1 to 3.

\textit{Step 3: The asymptote also shifts right by 2.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Asymptote: } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(x-2) \) is undefined when \( x \leq 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle  x \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  (3, 0) \); asymptote \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 32\quad\small\texttt{(el2-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 32 (el2-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The number of bacteria \( N \) in a culture after \( t \) hours satisfies \( N = 200e^{0.3t} \).  \\[3pt]  (a) Find the initial number of bacteria.  \\[3pt]  (b) Find the exact time when the number of bacteria reaches 2000.  \\[3pt]  (c) Give the time to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Set \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
N(0) = 200e^0 = 200
\end{gathered}\]
Initial count is 200 bacteria.

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Set \( N = 2000 \) and solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
200e^{0.3t} = 2000 \Rightarrow e^{0.3t} = 10
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 200.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.3t = \ln 10 \Rightarrow t = \frac{\ln 10}{0.3}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact solution.

\textit{Step 4: Part (c): Evaluate to 3 s.f.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln 10}{0.3} \approx 7.68 \text{ hours}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln 10 \approx 2.303 \Rightarrow \frac{2.303}{0.3} \approx 7.68 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 200. (b) \(\displaystyle  t = \dfrac{\ln 10}{0.3} \). (c) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 7.68 \) hours.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 33\quad\small\texttt{(el2-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 33 (el2-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A radioactive substance decays according to \( M = 500e^{-0.04t} \), where \( M \) is the mass in grams and \( t \) is the time in years.  \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial mass.  \\[3pt]  (b) Find the exact time for the mass to halve.  \\[3pt]  (c) Give the half-life to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Initial mass at \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M(0) = 500e^0 = 500 \text{ g}
\end{gathered}\]
The initial mass is 500 g.

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Set \( M = 250 \) (half of 500).}
\[\begin{gathered}
500e^{-0.04t} = 250 \Rightarrow e^{-0.04t} = 0.5
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 500.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.04t = \ln 0.5 = -\ln 2 \Rightarrow t = \frac{\ln 2}{0.04}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \( -0.04 \). The half-life formula.

\textit{Step 4: Part (c): Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln 2}{0.04} \approx 17.3 \text{ years}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln 2 \approx 0.6931 \Rightarrow \frac{0.6931}{0.04} \approx 17.3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 500 g. (b) \(\displaystyle  t = \dfrac{\ln 2}{0.04} \). (c) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 17.3 \) years.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 34\quad\small\texttt{(el2-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 34 (el2-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{(a) Solve \( e^{2x} = 50 \), giving an exact answer and a decimal answer to 3 s.f.  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (b) Solve \( \ln(x^2) = 6 \), giving exact answers.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = \ln 50 \Rightarrow x = \frac{\ln 50}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact solution.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate to 3 s.f.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\ln 50}{2} \approx \frac{3.912}{2} \approx 1.96
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln 50 \approx 3.912 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (b): \( \ln(x^2) = 6 \Rightarrow x^2 = e^6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = e^6
\end{gathered}\]
Raise \( e \) to both sides.

\textit{Step 4: Take both square roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \pm e^3
\end{gathered}\]
Both positive and negative values are valid since \( \ln(x^2) \) is defined for \( x \neq 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{\ln 50}{2} \approx 1.96 \). (b) \(\displaystyle  x = \pm e^3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 35\quad\small\texttt{(el2-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 35 (el2-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{(a) Given that \( \log_5 x = p \) and \( \log_5 y = 4p \), write \( y \) in terms of \( x \).  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (b) Given that \( \ln x = 2 \) and \( \ln y = -4 \), write \( y \) in terms of \( x \).  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (c) Solve \( e^{3x-1} = 8 \), giving an exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): From \( \log_5 x = p \): \( x = 5^p \Rightarrow 5 = x^{1/p} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5 = x^{1/p}
\end{gathered}\]
Express the base in terms of \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: From \( \log_5 y = 4p \): \( y = 5^{4p} \). Substitute \( 5 = x^{1/p} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \left(x^{1/p}\right)^{4p} = x^4
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{4p}{p} = 4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (b): From \( \ln x = 2 \): \( x = e^2 \Rightarrow e = x^{1/2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e = x^{1/2} = \sqrt{x}
\end{gathered}\]
Raise both sides of \( x = e^2 \) to \( \frac{1}{2} \).

\textit{Step 4: From \( \ln y = -4 \): \( y = e^{-4} \). Substitute.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \left(x^{1/2}\right)^{-4} = x^{-2} = \frac{1}{x^2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \).

\textit{Step 5: Part (c): Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x - 1 = \ln 8
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln e^{3x-1} = 3x - 1 \).

\textit{Step 6: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x = \ln 8 + 1 \Rightarrow x = \frac{\ln 8 + 1}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact answer. Note: \( \ln 8 = 3\ln 2 \), so \( x = \frac{3\ln 2 + 1}{3} = \ln 2 + \frac{1}{3} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  y = x^4 \). (b) \(\displaystyle  y = \dfrac{1}{x^2} \). (c) \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{\ln 8 + 1}{3} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 36\quad\small\texttt{(el2-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 36 (el2-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_2 32 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Find the power}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^5 = 32 \implies \log_2 32 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
By definition, \( \log_a x = y \Leftrightarrow a^y = x \), so we need the power of 2 that gives 32. Since \( 2^5 = 32 \), the logarithm is 5.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 5
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 37\quad\small\texttt{(el2-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 37 (el2-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_3 81 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Find the power}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^4 = 81 \implies \log_3 81 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_3 81 \) asks: what power of 3 gives 81? Listing powers \( 3^1=3,\ 3^2=9,\ 3^3=27,\ 3^4=81 \) shows the answer is 4.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 4
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 38\quad\small\texttt{(el2-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 38 (el2-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_{10} 0.001 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write as power of 10}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.001 = 10^{-3} \implies \log_{10} 0.001 = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Write the argument as a power of the base: \( 0.001 = \tfrac{1}{1000} = 10^{-3} \). A negative exponent gives a value between 0 and 1, so the logarithm is negative.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad -3
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 39\quad\small\texttt{(el2-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 39 (el2-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_5 \frac{1}{25} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write as power}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{25} = 5^{-2} \implies \log_5 \frac{1}{25} = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite the argument as a power of 5: \( \tfrac{1}{25} = \tfrac{1}{5^2} = 5^{-2} \). Reciprocals of the base give negative logarithms.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad -2
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 40\quad\small\texttt{(el2-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 40 (el2-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Write \( 3^4 = 81 \) in logarithmic form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 81 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the definition \( a^c = b \Leftrightarrow \log_a b = c \): the base of the power (3) becomes the base of the log, and the exponent (4) becomes the value of the log.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle log_3 81 = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 41\quad\small\texttt{(el2-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 41 (el2-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Write \( \log_2 64 = 6 \) in exponential form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^6 = 64
\end{gathered}\]
Reverse the log definition: \( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \). The base of the log (2) becomes the base of the power, and the value of the log (6) becomes the exponent.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 2^6 = 64\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 42\quad\small\texttt{(el2-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 42 (el2-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_4 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Any base to power 0 is 1}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^0 = 1 \implies \log_4 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
This is the key fact \( \log_a 1 = 0 \) for every valid base, because \( a^0 = 1 \) for any \( a > 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 0
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 43\quad\small\texttt{(el2-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 43 (el2-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_7 7 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{log\_a a = 1}
\[\begin{gathered}
7^1 = 7 \implies \log_7 7 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Another key fact: \( \log_a a = 1 \) for any valid base, because raising the base to the power 1 just gives the base back.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 44\quad\small\texttt{(el2-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 44 (el2-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \log_2 x = 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert to exponential}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2^5 = 32
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( \log_a x = y \Leftrightarrow a^y = x \) to rewrite the equation as a power. Here \( x = 2^5 = 32 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 32\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 45\quad\small\texttt{(el2-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 45 (el2-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \log_3 x = -2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert to exponential}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3^{-2} = \frac{1}{9}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the definition \( \log_a x = y \Leftrightarrow a^y = x \). A negative log means \( x \) is a reciprocal: \( 3^{-2} = \tfrac{1}{3^2} = \tfrac{1}{9} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 1/9\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 46\quad\small\texttt{(el2-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 46 (el2-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \log_x 49 = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 49 \implies x = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Positive base only.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 7\)
\end{answerbox}

\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Evaluate \( \ln e^4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{ln and e are inverses}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln e^4 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( \ln \) is \( \log_e \), the inverse property \( \log_a(a^x) = x \) gives \( \ln(e^4) = 4 \) directly.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 4
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 48\quad\small\texttt{(el2-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 48 (el2-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Evaluate \( e^{\ln 3} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{e and ln are inverses}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{\ln 3} = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The exponential and natural logarithm undo each other: \( e^{\ln x} = x \) for any \( x > 0 \). (Recall that \( \ln \) is only defined for positive arguments.)

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 3
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 49\quad\small\texttt{(el2-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 49 (el2-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \ln x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Exponentiate}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^2 \approx 7.39
\end{gathered}\]
Raise \( e \) to both sides to undo the natural log: \( e^{\ln x} = x \), so \( x = e^2 \). Numerically \( e^2 \approx 7.389 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = e^2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 50\quad\small\texttt{(el2-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 50 (el2-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 2^x = 10 \). Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \ln 2 = \ln 10 \implies x = \frac{\ln 10}{\ln 2} = 3.32
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides and use the power law \( \ln(a^x) = x\ln a \) to bring \( x \) down. Dividing gives the change-of-base form \( x = \dfrac{\ln 10}{\ln 2} = \log_2 10 \) --- change of base is exactly what lets you compute a logarithm with an awkward base on a calculator that only has \( \ln \) or \( \log_{10} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3.32\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 51\quad\small\texttt{(el2-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 51 (el2-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 5^x = 20 \). Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\ln 20}{\ln 5} = \frac{3.00}{1.609} = 1.86
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides, then use \( \ln(5^x) = x\ln 5 \) and divide. Note \( \ln 20 \approx 2.996 \), shown here rounded to \( 3.00 \) (3 s.f.) --- keep extra precision in intermediate steps to avoid rounding error in the final answer.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 1.86\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 52\quad\small\texttt{(el2-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 52 (el2-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 3^{2x-1} = 15 \). Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x-1)\ln 3 = \ln 15
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides. The power law \( \ln(a^k) = k\ln a \) brings the whole exponent \( 2x-1 \) down as a factor --- treat it as a single bracket to keep the algebra clean.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 1 = \frac{\ln 15}{\ln 3} = 2.465 \implies x = 1.73
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \( \ln 3 \) to isolate the linear expression in \( x \), then rearrange. Equivalently \( \dfrac{\ln 15}{\ln 3} = \log_3 15 \) by change of base.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 1.73\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 53\quad\small\texttt{(el2-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 53 (el2-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_8 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 2 as power of 8}
\[\begin{gathered}
8^{1/3} = 2 \implies \log_8 2 = \frac{1}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Ask what power of 8 gives 2. Since \( 2 = \sqrt[3]{8} = 8^{1/3} \), the log equals \( \tfrac{1}{3} \). Fractional logs always come from roots of the base.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1/3
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 54\quad\small\texttt{(el2-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 54 (el2-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_9 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 3 as power of 9}
\[\begin{gathered}
9^{1/2} = 3 \implies \log_9 3 = \frac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
A power of \( \tfrac{1}{2} \) is a square root, and \( \sqrt{9} = 3 \). So by the log definition, \( \log_9 3 = \tfrac{1}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1/2
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 55\quad\small\texttt{(el2-055)}}

\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\textit{Key features}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 56\quad\small\texttt{(el2-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 56 (el2-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = \log_{10} x \) and \( y = 10^x \) on the same axes. What is the relationship?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Inverse functions}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Reflections of each other in } y = x
\end{gathered}\]
\( y = \log_{10} x \) and \( y = 10^x \) are inverse functions, so their graphs are mirror images of each other in the line \( y = x \). The exponential passes through \( (0,1) \); the log passes through \( (1,0) \) --- these intercepts are reflections of each other.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Inverse functions; reflections in y = x
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 57\quad\small\texttt{(el2-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 57 (el2-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} = 8 \). Give exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = \ln 8 = 3\ln 2 \implies x = \frac{3\ln 2}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Natural log appears naturally because the base is \( e \): taking \( \ln \) of both sides uses the inverse property \( \ln(e^{2x}) = 2x \). Then \( \ln 8 = \ln(2^3) = 3\ln 2 \) by the power law, giving the tidiest exact form.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = (3 ln 2)/2\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic curve plotted with key points marked, and the dashed line through the solution.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 58\quad\small\texttt{(el2-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 58 (el2-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(2x + 1) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Exponentiate}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 = e^3 \implies x = \frac{e^3 - 1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Raise \( e \) to both sides to undo the \( \ln \): \( e^{\ln(2x+1)} = 2x+1 \). Then solve the resulting linear equation. (Check: \( 2x+1 = e^3 > 0 \), so the original \( \ln \) is defined.)

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = (e^3 - 1)/2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 59\quad\small\texttt{(el2-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 59 (el2-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log_2(x+3) + \log_2(x-1) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2[(x+3)(x-1)] = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Use the addition law \( \log_a P + \log_a Q = \log_a(PQ) \) to combine into a single log. Both arguments must be positive, so we need \( x > 1 \) --- keep this constraint in mind when picking valid roots.

\textit{Step 2: Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+3)(x-1) = 8 \implies x^2 + 2x - 11 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Convert to index form using \( \log_a M = c \Leftrightarrow M = a^c \), so \( (x+3)(x-1) = 2^3 = 8 \). Expand and rearrange to a quadratic.

\textit{Step 3: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{-2 + \sqrt{48}}{2} = -1 + 2\sqrt{3} \approx 2.46
\end{gathered}\]
Reject negative root (need x > 1).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = -1 + 2\sqrt{3}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 60\quad\small\texttt{(el2-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 60 (el2-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( \log_a a^5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a a^5 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the inverse property \( \log_a(a^x) = x \). Equivalently, by the power law \( \log_a(a^5) = 5\log_a a = 5 \times 1 = 5 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 5
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 61\quad\small\texttt{(el2-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 61 (el2-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{If \( \log_3 x = 2 \) and \( \log_3 y = 4 \), find \( \log_3(xy) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Addition law}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3(xy) = \log_3 x + \log_3 y = 2 + 4 = 6
\end{gathered}\]
The log of a product is the sum of the logs: \( \log_a(xy) = \log_a x + \log_a y \). This works only because both logs share the same base.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 6
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 62\quad\small\texttt{(el2-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 62 (el2-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 10^{x-1} = 500 \). Give answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take log10}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 1 = \log_{10} 500 = 2.699 \implies x = 3.70
\end{gathered}\]
Base-10 log is the natural choice when the base of the power is 10, because \( \log_{10}(10^{x-1}) = x-1 \) directly. Then add 1 to find \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3.70\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 63\quad\small\texttt{(el2-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 63 (el2-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Express \( 2\log a + 3\log b \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power then product}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log a^2 + \log b^3 = \log(a^2 b^3)
\end{gathered}\]
First use the power law \( k\log a = \log(a^k) \) to move each coefficient inside. Then combine using the addition law \( \log P + \log Q = \log(PQ) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle log(a^2 b^3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 64\quad\small\texttt{(el2-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 64 (el2-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Express \( \log 12 - \log 4 + \log 3 \) as a single logarithm and evaluate.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log\frac{12 \times 3}{4} = \log 9
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the addition law to the \( + \log \) terms (numerator) and the subtraction law to the \( - \log \) (denominator): \( \log A - \log B + \log C = \log\dfrac{AC}{B} \). The result simplifies to \( \log 9 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 9
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 65\quad\small\texttt{(el2-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 65 (el2-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log_5 x + \log_5 4 = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5(4x) = 2 \implies 4x = 25 \implies x = \frac{25}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Combine the two logs using the addition law, then convert to index form \( \log_5 M = 2 \Leftrightarrow M = 5^2 = 25 \). (Check \( x > 0 \): \( \tfrac{25}{4} > 0 \), so the original \( \log_5 x \) is defined.)

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 25/4\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 66\quad\small\texttt{(el2-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 66 (el2-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( \log_2 3 = 1.585 \), find \( \log_2 12 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 12 = 4 x 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 12 = \log_2 4 + \log_2 3 = 2 + 1.585 = 3.585
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise the argument so each piece is easy: \( 12 = 4 \times 3 \), then split via the addition law. \( \log_2 4 = 2 \) is exact, and \( \log_2 3 \) is the given value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 3.585
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 67\quad\small\texttt{(el2-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 67 (el2-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^x + e^{-x} = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
u + \frac{1}{u} = 3 \implies u^2 - 3u + 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( u = e^x \); then \( e^{-x} = \tfrac{1}{u} \). Multiplying through by \( u \) (which is positive since \( e^x > 0 \), so no extraneous roots) clears the fraction and gives a quadratic in \( u \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
u = \frac{3 \pm \sqrt{5}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the quadratic formula. Both roots are positive (since \( \sqrt{5} < 3 \)), so both are valid values for \( u = e^x \) --- important because \( \ln \) is only defined for positive arguments.

\textit{Step 3: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln\left(\frac{3 + \sqrt{5}}{2}\right) \text{ or } x = \ln\left(\frac{3 - \sqrt{5}}{2}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Both positive so both valid.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = ln((3 + sqrt5)/2) or x = ln((3 - sqrt5)/2)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Logarithms 68\quad\small\texttt{(el2-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 68 (el2-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The Richter scale: an earthquake of magnitude \( M \) has energy \( E = 10^{1.5M+4.8} \) joules. Find M when \( E = 10^{12} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Set equal}
\[\begin{gathered}
1.5M + 4.8 = 12 \implies 1.5M = 7.2 \implies M = 4.8
\end{gathered}\]
Since both sides are powers of 10 with the same base, the exponents must be equal: \( 10^{a} = 10^{b} \Rightarrow a = b \). This avoids needing logs at all when both sides share a base.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle M = 4.8\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 69\quad\small\texttt{(el2-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 69 (el2-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Use the change of base formula to evaluate \( \log_3 20 \) to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Change of base}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 20 = \frac{\ln 20}{\ln 3} = \frac{3.00}{1.099} = 2.73
\end{gathered}\]
Change of base: \( \log_a x = \dfrac{\ln x}{\ln a} \) (or any other common base such as \( \log_{10} \)). This is essential because most calculators only have \( \ln \) and \( \log_{10} \) buttons, not arbitrary bases.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 2.73
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic curve plotted with key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Logarithms 70\quad\small\texttt{(el2-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Logarithms 70 (el2-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( \log_2 x + \log_4 x = 6 \). [Hint: use change of base.]}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Change base of log\_4 x}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4 x = \frac{\log_2 x}{\log_2 4} = \frac{\log_2 x}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
The two logs have different bases, so we cannot combine them directly. Use change of base \( \log_a x = \dfrac{\log_b x}{\log_b a} \) with \( b = 2 \) to rewrite \( \log_4 x \) in terms of \( \log_2 x \); since \( \log_2 4 = 2 \), this halves it.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 x + \frac{\log_2 x}{2} = 6 \implies \frac{3}{2}\log_2 x = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Both terms are now in the same base, so collect them. Treating \( \log_2 x \) as a single unknown, \( 1 + \tfrac{1}{2} = \tfrac{3}{2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 x = 4 \implies x = 16
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( \tfrac{2}{3} \) to isolate \( \log_2 x \), then convert to index form \( x = 2^4 = 16 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 16\)
\end{answerbox}
